ParlaMint-DK_20210520100656	No amendments have been tabled. Does anyone wish to speak? Mrs Pernille Vermund does. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210520100718	Thank you. Back in March, New Citizens' proposal to abolish the right to seek spontaneous asylum in Denmark was first examined here in the Danish Parliament. Unfortunately, only DF indicated that they would vote in favour. Now is the day for us to vote on the proposal. Unfortunately, I have no expectations that the other parties have changed their minds, but I am nevertheless speaking in the hope that I can wake up the rest of the Folketing here today. In recent days, a record number of migrants have illegally crossed the border with the Spanish enclave Ceuta on the coast of Morocco in North Africa. More than 8,000 migrants have swam, wandered or sailed in inflatable boats. According to TV 2's correspondent in Spain, the situation is chaotic and there is no doubt that the many migrants will try to reach the rest of Europe through Spain. I know that the number of asylum seekers in Denmark is low as a result of the corona crisis and as a result of the legally closed borders which the crisis brought about. But it can't lull us to sleep. Denmark is exactly as unprotected today as we did during the great failure of autumn 2015, when thousands of migrants wandered across Europe and across our borders. That is why we need to exercise due care and we need to abolish access to come here by travelling through several safe countries and applying for spontaneous asylum in Denmark. Because before we know it, southern Europe can again be crossed by migrants who will set their course for Denmark in the north. And we will not be guarded against it; the next wave of migrants will hit us as hard as it did during the migrant crisis in 2015 and 2016. There is now a broad common understanding here in the Folketing that the current asylum policy is dysfunctional and inhumane; that it supports human trafficking and helps the few at the expense of the many; that it acts as a magnet for migrants who do not share our values, do not comply with our laws and who directly oppose our freedom, democracy and popular rule; and that it inflicts great damage on Danish society and costs the Danish taxpayers a great deal of money. In fact, we could help far more people fleeing for much less money if we only helped real refugees in the neighbourhood. We don't. Today, we are glorifying the few at the expense of the many, typically young, strong men who are driven through a purgatory of traffickers across the Mediterranean and up through Europe at the expense of women, children and the elderly who are left in camps and who do not get help because we give priority to spending the funds on the few who come up here. There are so many good arguments for managing who we are letting into our country, and that requires a stop to spontaneous asylum. It can even be made relatively simple with few legislative changes without conflicting with our international conventions. So if the Folketing had the courage and the will, in the vote in a few minutes or perhaps just one minute, we could decide to abolish the right to seek spontaneous asylum in Denmark. But that courage and that will here Parliament unfortunately doesn't bother me. From New Citizens' perspective, our primary task is to look after Denmark and to look after the Danes. We owe that to previous generations, we owe it to the generations of Danes who are going to come after us. I think it is worth remembering that for every year that goes by, for every migrant who comes here, who does not bring anything positive with him, but who harms our society, then you hurt Denmark and not good. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210520104002	The sitting is resumed. The debate is open. First, it's the Minister for Gender Equality.
ParlaMint-DK_20210520104028	Thank you very much for the floor. With the motion for a resolution that we are debating today, the proposers will give everyone access to free menstrual products. I would like to thank the proposers for giving us the opportunity to talk about menstruation and priorities on how best to achieve equality in Denmark. Let me start by saying that the government believes that there are more equality problems to address than free menstrual products. We give priority to areas that can put real, lasting tracks in society and promote gender equality broadly. Having said that, I would like to acknowledge that there may be challenges in tabooing menstrual periods. It can be difficult to talk about, and some may feel that it is shameful. That is why I think it is good that we are taking that debate in the Folketing. For menstruation is a completely natural part of life and the prerequisite for creating life. No one should feel ashamed or disgusting about having a menstrual period. It's a natural part of life. It has to be clear. I want everyone to know that. However, in my opinion, we do not solve challenges with taboos and shame by making menstrual products free of charge. The authors shall state the reasons on which they are based, inter alia: the proposal that menstrual products are an additional cost, which for some may be a significant economic challenge. But providing free products is simply not the way the Danish welfare system works. In Denmark, we have a wide-ranging welfare society with a variety of services for different target groups, e.g.: Social security, cash benefits, sickness benefit and pension. The various social security benefits have different purposes, such as: reflects whether it is a temporary or more permanent liability. However, the individual payments are fixed in such a way that they can meet the basic needs of the citizen and they are the same for men and women. It is up to the citizen himself to give priority to his consumer choice. Of course, there are services aimed at citizens in certain life situations. For example: there are higher benefits to parents and especially single parents who take into account the additional costs associated with having children. But basically, it is up to the citizens themselves to decide how they will dispose of their money. I do not think that we politicians should interfere in how people spend their money. But we must of course make sure that you have a reasonable income if you cannot support yourself for a period of time. In addition, the proposal also raises the question of how we should prioritise our common social resources and the resources of the Finance Act. For me, it is crucial that we prioritise and invest in getting well out on the other side of the corona crisis. I do not wish to deny that free menstrual products are the right solution to equality problems in Scotland. In Denmark, I believe there are other and greater gender equality challenges that we must prioritise and address. One of the priorities of the government is the prevention of sexual harassment, the fight against negative social control and better opportunities for fathers to take leave with their children. It is about structural changes that put real ground in society and promote gender equality widely. For the government, equality is an important and common struggle for everyone to really have equal opportunities. That is why the government cannot support the proposal put forward. Thank you for the floor.

This means that it is obviously difficult for many to get rid of their debts. We would really like to take a closer look at this. Because the real possibility of debt cancellation is non-existent, in fact, we can see that in the few cases where it is cancelled one of the most frequent reasons why the debt is cancelled is that it dies. You simply die from your debt. And it is not because I do not also agree that when you have committed crime, you have to take the penalty, and most people do. The servings, like Christian did, they go through rehab programs, just as Christian did, they take an education, just as Christian is doing, and they have the same dream that Christian has right now, namely a dream of being debt-free at another time. And when I meet people like Christian, they are always full of hope when they face a release: Now it has to be, now they will put the crime behind them, now they will start afresh. They mean it, they hope it, and they believe it. But as soon as they are released, the problems are towering. Then they may need a home, and then they find out that hope breaks when the criminal record blocks them from getting into a job, and when the bill from criminal costs ticks in, they find out that they have a lifelong debt. And in fact, the two things most frequently mentioned to me by the inmates are barriers to moving forward with their lives, precisely criminal costs and criminal records. So what do we know about the groups that are facing this debt afterwards? In fact, researcher Annette Olesen has dealt with this a little, and she says that there are actually three groups: there is a group that does manage in spite. They're gonna be hard, but they're gonna start from the end. It is not certain that they will ever be free of debt, but at least they will not do more crime. Others may be a little more indifferent to it. They know they're going out and doing crime again. That's what they can do, and maybe they have an abuse, maybe they have a weak network. They are typically very vulnerable, and I guess they're in that group of 72 percent. of the debtors who are not liable and who have no ability to pay, but who account for 80%. of the debt arising from criminal proceedings costs. It is not certain that there can be agreement on this, but in reality we could just as well cancel this group's debts • we will never get money from them; they have no ability to pay; they are so exposed that it will never happen alone to avoid using the many resources we do in public systems to deal with a debt that will never come back. But then there is the middle group, and I think it is really interesting in this context: they really want to stop their crime, but what we know about them is that when adversity gets too great, there is also a great risk that they will fall back, and this is where I really wish we had some tools to maintain good development and motivate them by putting up some opportunities for them. Maybe we could give it that carrot by putting them in perspective that they could get out of this debt within a reasonable period of time. They would be given some incentives to move forward, a hope, and best of all: if we succeed, this will result in fewer victims, at least fewer new victims. So you have to ask yourself: what is the real problem with this system of criminal proceedings as it is today? I think the first problem is that this is poverty-creating, because it maintains people in a hopeless debt situation without the possibility of debt relief. I have used the example in the past that if a director goes stupid and goes bankrupt, in principle I know that it isn't always so easy to get access to debt relief, but why is it that you can't also have the view of other people that during a period of their lives they can fool themselves, commit crime and become smarter and experience some kind of accommodation, so that we also help them? In 1974 an almost unanimous Parliament abolished what was called service costs. Every day they paid for residence, board and lodging in the institutions of the Department of Justice, and the reason why it was abolished was first of all that it was recognised that the huge debt situation when you were released was a problem, and then it was also enormously resourceful for the police to administer, and there was an uneven procedure and so on. So it was decided to do away with it. I think the Progress Party was against, but it was because they would rather pay the prisoners a collective wage for the work they did in prisons and then they could pay by means of it. So the reason for the abolition was, of course, a great deal about debt counteracting rehabilitation, and there is, in principle, not the great difference between the costs of criminal proceedings that we have today. So you already knew back then that it's bad when people start a life with a debt and that it causes a problem. Secondly, this group of people who have this debt is experiencing visits by the bailiff and the police, who are constantly confiscating the valuables that can repay the debt, and that means that you can never save for anything and enjoy it, because it can disappear tomorrow. You can't own a car, you can't enter into a house store, and researcher Annette Olesen actually says that many so choose to have a different address for having the opportunity to maintain a life. But we also know that if people are going to have to have a stable base and that they have to have a home, and you can actually get and we have seen examples of that, says Annette Olesen, they will be stopped on their way to the airport on their way to vacation, and if you bring cash for the holiday, they can be confiscated, and then you will be there with the whole family. That's pretty crazy. Thirdly, however, we risk wasting all the work we do to get people out of crime. Because if you are fortunate enough to find a job, many actually end up at a cash aid rate when the payments are paid, and if that is something that you can look forward to for the rest of your life, then perhaps motivation goes. I guess you understand that. Because if you can't change your situation yourself, you might as well go to cash instead of sticking to a job. In fact, we should make a calculation of what we here lose from tax revenue on people who then never get into jobs. Fourthly, we hardly ever recover anything. Seventy-two percent. As I said, 80%. of the debt, but is completely unpayable, so it's small amounts that are recovered, and that means that it's almost free to make this proposal. You don't get the debt canceled either. We can see this from the fact that the amount of remission is incredibly low. There is an element of chance lottery over it, because it can only happen after a very long time, and then the sense of justice, which is also an element of canceling debt, is not even a uniform size to navigate after. And if you are lucky enough to get forgiveness, it happens in practice after really many years of being held in a hopeless debt, and the point is, of course, that you really need that push closer to release in order to maintain a life free from crime. Then there is the legal security problem of the judge declaring criminal costs at a time when the bill has not been established. It may well be that it has been calculated for the lawyers, but it has not been calculated for the police, and no one can answer what the practice is for the police's assessment, what it is they count with, why and how. We simply cannot get an answer to that. So there is also something legal about how we deal with this kind of debt; at least it is in a way that we would never accept in civil law. So I think there is a basis for looking at these rules, and I am glad that to some extent there is support for it, and I also think that is why we have all received a letter from more than 20 organisations and people who support the need for something to happen before today's debate. So I have a hope that that will be the case. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210520132511	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister of Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20210520132534	Thank you to the Danish People's Party for tabling a motion for a resolution which deals with a subject very dear to the government, namely the question of the theft and abuse of the identity of others. There is no doubt that being subjected to identity theft or abuse of identity is very unpleasant and offensive to the victim. This is true even if identity theft does not as such take place in order to achieve economic gain, for example. if you have abused your private photos and identity on Facebook, on Instagram or for that matter on the Tinder dating app, which we have unfortunately seen examples of. Today it is a crime to abuse someone else's identity when it comes to committing another crime. Identity theft can, for example: be punishable under criminal law provisions on fraud where this is done in order to obtain unjustified financial gain or in the case of unjustified disclosure of information relating to someone else's private affairs. But it is not in itself a criminal offence under the criminal code to pretend to be someone else, for example. by creating a social media profile that appears as another person's profile. We in the government believe that something needs to be done about this, and we therefore fully agree with that part of the resolution tabled by the Danish People's Party. We have therefore also launched, together with the SF, Radicals and Enhedslisten, an initiative for a separate ban on identity theft, even if no illegal action is committed following it. It must in future be criminally unjustified to impersonate another person by using his or her identity information, pictures or the like. Crime will therefore not require identity theft to be carried out for a specific purpose, for example. to damage the reputation of the person concerned or to achieve financial gain. I expect to present the bill before the end of the current calendar year. The reason why we cannot support the motion for a resolution today is that, in addition to criminalising identity theft, the motion by the Danish People's Party also calls for criminalising the use of other people's images when it is not for the purpose of impersonating someone else, in other words in cases where there is no question of theft or misuse of their identity. It can, for example, be done. where appropriate, when another person's image is used on a poster or similar for a festive occasion, or for that matter when a news media brings a picture of a person to a news article without the consent of that person. I believe that this is generally somewhat less offensive and therefore less of a punishment than identity theft. A criminal code on identity theft should therefore, in the view of the government, be reserved for cases where the unjustified use of the image implies that the perpetrator somehow pretends to be the person who appears in the picture. If there is only an unjustified use of images, I believe that their regulation is more natural in the case of special legislation than in criminal law, including for example. in the Data Protection Act or in the Intellectual Property Rights Act. So the government cannot therefore support the resolution. However, I would like to thank the authors of the motion for a resolution and for stressing the importance of combating identity theft and abuse of identity. Thank you for the floor.

These are evidently the terms of the current legislation. That is why we have also asked the Minister in the past to clarify what rules apply in this area. According to the reply from the Ministry of Justice, the processing of personal data arising from the creation of false profiles with another person's name or image could constitute an infringement of the Data Protection Regulation and the Data Protection Act. Such an infringement may, according to the Ministry, be punishable by a fine or a prison sentence, as the case may be. In addition, there are a number of provisions in the criminal code which criminalise the misuse of identities when it comes to committing other crime. Identity abuse can, for example: be punishable under Article 279 of the Criminal Code on fraud when it is done in order to obtain unjustified financial gain. In addition, misuse of identity may, for example, be punishable in the case of hacking or breach of the secret of correspondence under Article 263 of the Criminal Code. However, the penal code does not cover all cases of misuse of identities. For example, it will not in itself be punishable by criminal law to pretend to be someone else, including creating a facebook profile or a blog in someone else's name. And then we're back at the starting point. Because, of course, impersonating someone else must be punishable by misusing another person's identity and images and thus potentially violating or defaming them. The damage that can be done to people who are injured before they become aware of the identity theft and possibly stop it, if at all possible, is not an offence that must be tolerated. It's not an injury you just have to tolerate. As may be seen from my rapporteur's speech, I have to say that when we received this proposal from DF, it seemed very delicate, but we naturally support the proposal. I am not sure that the rapporteur for the proposal here is aware of the matter I am referring to, but if the proposer is not, then that is quite fine. ParlaMint-DK_20210520144438	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Minister for Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20210520144455	Thank you for the floor. Let me start by saying that I fully agree with the Danish People's Party that gang crime is a major problem. The band shootings, disrespectful behavior and terrorisation of urban space pose a threat to security and security in Denmark and we take this seriously. The fight against gang crime is a key priority for the government, and we are willing to go a long way to get rid of the gangs. The motion for a resolution deals with the residence bans which can be imposed by judgment on persons who have committed gang crimes. The scheme is an important tool in the fight against the gangs; it provides an opportunity to address the insecurity created locally by the presence and crime of the gangs. The motion for a resolution proposes, among other things, that a violation of the residence ban should trigger a 60-day sentence in prison in first cases, while second- and third-time offences should trigger a doubling and a triple of the length of the sentence and so on. In addition, a number of other proposals for amendments, such as: on the extension of the scope of the residence ban. I would like to sign for the proposal. We must constantly consider whether our tools in the fight against the gangs are good enough. I share the view that it should trigger a significant and consistent punishment if gang criminals violate a residence ban, and that is actually the case today. As far as the proposed levels of penalties are concerned, it is already the case today that, in first cases, a violation of the residence ban should, as a starting point, trigger 60 days of unconditional imprisonment. The legal practice in this area is that it is still scarce, but according to the Attorney General, it can be linked to the fact that the rules are from 2017 and many of the people who have been sentenced to a residence ban in connection with gang-related crime continue to be served. However, the starting point for the level of punishment seems to be followed by the courts. The few final decisions we have in this area show that one violation of a residence ban gives 60 days of unconditional imprisonment; two violations give 3 months of unconditional imprisonment and three violations give 4-5 months of unconditional imprisonment. So it is not as far away from what is being proposed. As I said, it is also proposed to extend the scope of the residence ban so that all gang members can be sentenced to a residence ban, not just those belonging to a gang in conflict. This is another point in which the motion for a resolution is, in fact, already partially accepted by the rules in force. It is indeed the case that the rules already provide for the possibility, under certain conditions, of imposing a residence ban on persons associated with gangs where the crime leading to the residence ban is related to the convicted person's association with a gang group, regardless of whether the group is in conflict. Having said that, I am certainly open to looking at whether the scheme is working properly and whether we can further strengthen the rules. I would just like to point out that it follows from the preparatory work to the law that an evaluation of the residence ban should be carried out when the scheme has been in force for four years, i.e. until December of this year. At the same time, with the agreement on the police and prosecutor's finances, we have committed ourselves to providing the gang packages with a service to see whether the measures that have been taken are now sufficient. In this context, we will also have to evaluate the system of residence bans. I therefore believe that it makes the best sense to look at the need for possible changes to the scheme in the light of the evaluation that will be carried out in the framework of the service review, as agreed in the multiannual agreement. In view of this, the government cannot support the proposal. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210520151648	That is actually a very good proposal. And now I have to move on to how it can be that we cannot support it anyway. The proposal falls into two parts. See, that exists in relation to the first part of this with: How do we deal with inmates who commit crime during serving? Then there is the existence of a crime, and this is contrary to what is suggested in the motion for a resolution....................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................... It is actually the case that if you commit a criminal offence in the prison, as if you commit a criminal offence outside the prison, then it will be reported to the police and dealt with in accordance with the general rules of the criminal code. In the event of disciplinary action, it shall be dealt with in accordance with the disciplinary rules. If a relationship breaks both sets of rules, it will be sanctioned according to both sets of rules. So we are in a situation where, in relation to what is proposed, it is that punishment should be extended when committing something criminal during service is such that matters are reported to the police and that the courts decide whether or not to be punished. So in this way it can be said that the first part of the proposal proposes that we should make the regulation in Denmark that applies in Denmark. Then there is the second part of the proposal, namely that the term of service for crimes committed during service should be immediately followed. There we really have to look at the criminal justice chain in its entirety. Because we cannot guarantee that a judgment can be served immediately, because the case has to go through the criminal proceedings chain. Let us take an example: a new crime is committed by an inmate shortly before the release, it is reported to the police, the case is prosecuted, the sentence is handed down and there is to be served. But the person concerned will be released by then. Then you could just say, 'Well, then we'll make the rules so that it can be done.' But how would you change the rules? Then we would have to change the rules so that we said: those conditions that are committed when you are in service must be given priority in the criminal proceedings chain. As you know, we have other priorities, which we have agreed in the police agreement: dangerous crime and so on. we provide special treatment times for. However, if we were to say that these conditions have to be put right in the criminal proceedings chain, so that we are sure that they will be dealt with quickly, it would mean that with the resources we have at our disposal there would be some other cases that would be pushed further behind. And will we say that whoever commits a criminal offence during serving must be given priority over the person who commits a rape or the person who commits violence or any of the other serious crimes? I really don't think any of us would. And that is why I believe that all parties in the Folketing will say in broad terms: this is a good and relevant debate. The motion for a resolution that has been tabled should not be supported, because the first part is as it is today, and that is fine. And the second part will have some unfortunate consequences, because we are going to give priority to some crime to serve on some other crime, which we may, as I know the parties widely, really do not want to do. Thank you.

So very briefly: if you are in the care of society, you must, of course, continue to be punished, if you continue to commit crime when you are in the care of society. That is why we propose that every form of crime you commit, even when you have been punished behind the walls of the prison, should be punished for a judge and punished separately for it, and that you should serve the sentence directly, in addition to the sentence that you have the prospect of serving or already serving. This means that there is nothing to do with the fact that because you are already serving and you are already being punished for something, you cannot be convicted of a new relationship. And if you're really unlucky from the point of view of society, you actually see that you just get off with a disciplinary sentence because you say: well, yes, but it's only something that comes later in connection with this attack, so why don't we just settle for disciplinary punishment? And it's a very weak signal to send if you, as an inmate, make crime when you're in prison and are told that well, yes, you can just get a little disciplinary thing here 1 day or 2 days, and that's it. It is a very weak signal to send, not least to gang criminals. So, inmates in Danish prisons must behave properly. They don't want to commit crime, and if they can't stay away from it, they're gonna have to get past a judge and get a second sentence. I guess that's not too much to ask. So I do not think, regardless of what the Minister of Justice makes of very logical considerations, and we are all convinced when the Minister is finished, as was said, that there is anyone outside Christiansborg's thick walls who understands something about the possibility of committing crime, for example. Assault an employee while you're in jail, and then actually let go of the fact that it's probably something you're looking at for a long time. ParlaMint-DK_20210520154545	I would point out that the vote on any proposals for adoption will be postponed until Tuesday 25 May 2021. The first to speak is the rapporteur for the authors of the motion, Mr von der Vring. Søren Søndergaard from Enhedslisten, for a reason for the query.
ParlaMint-DK_20210520154627	Thank you. The night to 7 April 1990 the passenger ship burned the Scandinavian Star on the way from Oslo to Copenhagen. The fire killed 159 people and at least one unborn child. There is much we do not know about that tragedy, but there are also things we know for sure. We know that at least the first two fires were set by one or more people on board. We know that the person or persons responsible for the fires have not been found and that we are therefore faced with Scandinavia's biggest unsolved assassination fire since World War II. We know that the ship was in exceptionally bad condition and should never have sailed with passengers. We know that the ship had sailed on the United States, where it had also been hit by mysterious fires. We know that part of the original crew followed the ship from the United States to Scandinavia. We know that the ship was sold several times shortly before the fire and that the sale price was doubled last time in 18 minutes. The same applies to the amount of insurance. We know that the Danish owner who had bought the ship returned the check to the original owner, who also received the insurance sum. We now officially know, thanks to the Justice Minister's correction to the Parliamentary Committee on Legal Affairs on 12 October 2020 and other answers, that there has never been a police investigation into the ownership, nesting and insurance conditions in relation to the assassination fire itself, but only under the law on ship safety at sea, i.e. the miserable security conditions on board. In short, we are faced with an unsolved assassination fire with enormous human consequences that have never been fully investigated by the police. This is why a number of parties have joined forces on this request, asking the government to provide information on the status of the investigation in connection with the assassination fire at Scandinavian Star and, as a result, to indicate what the government intends to do in order for a clarification to come closer. We look forward to the Justice Minister's reply.

ParlaMint-DK_20210520165252	That's fine. I'm happy to do that. Thank you to the Left for raising this query debate, which deals with an important subject, namely dental care for the weakened elderly. I will begin my speech by telling you about the target group for care care. The target group for this dental care scheme is people who, due to reduced mobility or excessive physical or mental disability, can only be difficult to use the ordinary dental care services. So this is not explicitly a dental care scheme targeted at older citizens. In practice, it is often them, I know that, but that is how the system is introduced and described. It is not intended that a wider circle of infirm elderly citizens or persons with disabilities who can be treated in ordinary dental clinics with disabilities should be included in the care provision, nor should care care be directed at persons who can use the existing dental care services but who for various reasons have chosen not to do so. Having said that, the majority of carers are of course still elderly people who receive an old age pension. Because of limited self - care and reduced ability to function, the majority of the population is in great need of personal and practical help in everyday life. This is also why a significant proportion of elderly people staying in nursing or elderly homes or in nursing homes meet the search criteria for care. Older people living in their own homes and receiving extensive help from home care and from home nurses represent a large proportion of the carers' family. If you look at the target group for care care, it is made up of citizens who most often have more health problems. It could be, for example. chronic diseases, it may be mental impairment, dementia, it may also be, and that it may affect the elderly's desire or ability to receive dental treatment. And, of course, efforts to ensure good oral hygiene and dental health for the individual citizen must be organised taking into account the situation of the individual citizen. This scheme has a few years on the back, and in 2014 a service review was carried out on the requirements of the Health Act for the content of municipal care. In this service inspection it was, among other things. concluded that the municipalities could probably strengthen the outreach effort and that there was a relatively low level of knowledge and connection to the scheme. This could be due to the fact that not all patients in the target group are able to seek care information. It almost goes without saying. Some recommendations were also made in the service inspection: a guide is prepared for visitation to care services; a guide for mobile dental care is prepared in your own home; the availability of special dental clinics and the conditions of transport to dental care are examined; and the dental health of patients in care care is recorded in the central Odontological Register of the Danish Health Authority, the SCOR, for the purpose of planning and evaluating care care care. In 2016, the Danish Health Service then prepared the report "Modernisation of care care care" just as a follow-up to this service review some years earlier. In that report, if you throw yourself into it, there are also a number of recommendations, especially about the visitation, and that is also what I can sense this question is based on. Here the Danish Health Authority recommends, among other things: In particular, municipalities develop and maintain citizen-oriented information material and develop common guidelines on how health care professionals, practitioners, practitioners, dental practitioners, dental practitioners, dental practitioners and care professionals can refer patients to care care services and, finally, that municipalities continuously evaluate their visitation practices for care care, for example. based on knowledge of how many citizens of the municipality are deemed to need care provision. Now let's see what's happened since those recommendations came in. KL has informed, and it is an answer I have sent to the Danish Parliament this March this year, that since the survey that was in 2016, when it was pointed out that there was not enough searching for care care, the focus has been on strengthening visitation to care in KL. KL has written out on this subject to all municipalities with an invitation to communicate knowledge about offers to staff at care centres and in home care. Then we have also informed that in 2016-2018 a project called "Culture changes in the elderly" was carried out: Dental and oral hygiene, and the overall purpose was to train staff in the care of the elderly to assist the elderly in dental hygiene. KL states that the project led to better foot health for the elderly in the participating municipalities and a stronger cooperation between care and care staff. KL also states that in OK-21 funds have been set aside to identify how the framework for dental practitioners to be included in the multi-disciplinary health care of older citizens can be strengthened. It is clear that it is absolutely essential for the municipalities to ensure that all citizens who are in the target group to receive care and who, incidentally, want it, are also offered. It is also important to remember that visitation to care care is based on a concrete and individual assessment of the individual citizen. I would like to state that the Ministry will ensure that follow-up is given to the Health Authority's recommendations for modernising care care from 2016, including, as far as visitation is concerned, whether the recommendations have been followed in the municipalities. Thank you, President.

ParlaMint-DK_20210520175803	This resolution has emerged from a citizens' proposal. The debate is open. The Secretary of State for Taxation.
ParlaMint-DK_20210520175853	Thank you. The citizens' proposal proposes that the Danish Parliament should create a wage reform to replace the civil service reform and that the reform should be based, for example, on: responsibilities and length of training, and takes into account tasks between professional groups and remuneration. It is also proposed that Parliament should present a plan for achieving this objective within a clear timeframe. The reason for this proposal is that there is a considerable shortfall in pay for public employees in women's dominated professions and that this is due to the pay hierarchy established by the Danish Parliament and to the reform of civil servants in 1969. Wages and other conditions for several of the groups mentioned in the proposal were already agreed before 1969 in agreements between the two sides of industry. At the same time, salaries and other conditions have been negotiated and agreed between the same parties over the last 50 years and subsequently approved through democratic votes among Members. Before going further in this speech, I would like to say that the government and I are, of course, deeply concerned about the issue of equality and equal pay. We are, of course, also concerned with the public sector, where equality and equal pay for all categories of staff are very close to our hearts. In particular, the reform of the staff required that in future the salaries of officials should be determined by collective agreement between the minister concerned and the civil servants' central bodies. The salaries laid down in the civil service reform were based on a report from the Staff Commission, which was temporary and was only to apply until agreement was reached. Since 1969, wages and other conditions have been negotiated, in accordance with the reform, between the parties every two or three years in the field of civil servants, and it is under these agreements that changes have been agreed in relation to the salary established by the reform of civil servants. In addition, a large number of civil servants, including nurses and midwives, have, if you like, been closed down. This means that new recruitments have then been made on contractual terms with the wage systems agreed by the parties in the agreements. These agreed wages have therefore replaced the previous statutory salaries, and they must be seen as an expression of the parties' priorities through negotiations over the last 50 years. There are many different analyses of wage differences in the public labour market. It is a complex subject with a great many nuances, and it was documented, for example, in the more than 1,000-page report from the Salaries Commission in 2010, where both experts and the social partners participated. In the government, of course, we listen to all the relevant input, including the views and wishes of the interested parties, in the current debate. It is the government's clear view that the salaries of public servants, as well as those of the rest of the labour market, should be determined by agreements and agreements between the parties in the field in question. Only in this way can agreements be based on the current priorities of the parties, including internally between the trade unions. In view of developments since 1969, when agreements and agreements have then also replaced legislation, the Government considers that it makes no sense to repeal the civil service reform as such. In conclusion, I would like to stress that the government wants to safeguard the Danish model, in which the parties lay down terms and conditions of employment. The proposal will potentially also have far-reaching consequences, including for the parties' encouragement to seek and compromise in the future. Finally, I would like to say that the government does not share the pessimistic view of which issues can be dealt with within the Danish model, as the proposal also expresses. On the contrary, we in the government have great faith in the strength of the Danish model, which has, in fact, repeatedly demonstrated its ability to deal with very difficult issues. The main organization of the trade union movement has just proposed the setting up of a remuneration structure committee after the final conclusion of collective bargaining. The Committee must analyse the wage structure in the public labour market, and this is a very good idea for the government. The Government will therefore, on the basis of the contribution of the main trade union organisation, continue to develop a mandate as a follow-up. Against this background, the government cannot support this motion for a resolution.

A staff assessment board was set up to initiate and coordinate the use of job descriptions and systematic assessment and assessment of staff. So the Council had to look at whether something was wrong. The problem is, that advice never really worked. I am only aware of two examples of the Council's activity: in 1982 the salaries of ordinary police officers were adjusted, and in 1985 there was an upward adjustment of the salaries of a group of public leaders. By the way, the Council was suspended in 2001, so we cannot blame it for not doing anything today. However, it points out that one was well aware that there would be a need to adjust continuously. After all, nobody says that there are no differences and that there are no differences that should be addressed. It's just about how we get to grips with it. I think it would be very good if, in the light of the debate here today, we had been able to write a joint contribution to the report, in which we recognised the imbalance and in which we declared that we would work to ensure that the recommendations were actually picked up. ParlaMint-DK_20210520195020	First of all, I would like to say first of all that the Free Greens naturally support the citizens' proposal. So thank you in the gallery, thank you to the 65 000 citizens who have made sure that we now deal with the proposal in here. In fact, the Free Greens themselves had a proposal on today's agenda before the citizens' proposal was dealt with, which was also about the civil service reform, but we then drew it so that we could have a staff reform from 1969 is a distorted foundation which forms the basis for wage developments in the public sector. It is an unequal structure that we build our society on the society in which we constantly praise and even to take the lead in equality. It is a reform that dates from a time when women did not have their own tax return and where their income could be seen as a small addition to their husbands. In the Commission's work, which laid the foundations for the reform of the civil service, it was decided to continue broadly the pay hierarchy which was already in force at that time. In other words, the wage classification of professional groups in the public sector, which forms the basis for the wage negotiations today, and to a large extent sets the framework for the wage negotiations in the collective bargaining process, is, in fact, largely continuing the valuation of traditional women's work which was carried out in 1919. So it was four years after women were given the right to vote. The pay hierarchy of traditional female occupations, such as midwife and nurse, reflects a view of women over 100 years of age. But I think we can all agree in here today that a lot has happened since 1919 and 1969. Yet women today earn less than men and this creates structural inequality in both income and pension. Women earn on average approx. 13% less an hour than men. This is due both to the pay gap between men and women working in the same workplace, but it is also due to the generally lower wage paid to the occupational groups where mainly women work. Statistics speak their own clear language. We need legislation and a labour market that goes with time and promotes equality. As this citizens' proposal, which has now obtained more than 65,000 signatures, shows, there is huge popular support to offset the pay gap between men and women and to support the fair pay of public employees in traditional women's professions. It is a struggle for equal pay that has been going on for many, many years, and it is a battle that has experienced a crazy boom over the last six months, not least thanks to you up in the gallery. Activists arrange demonstrations around the country and there are campaigns on social media. They demand that in here we take on our responsibilities to clean up history and correct the crooked foundations on which the collective bargaining is based. In other words, there's great momentum to actually move on this. We owe this to the civil servants, and we owe it to ourselves as a society. That is why, of course, we are in favour of making up the pay gap between the traditionally female-dominated and traditionally male-dominated professions in the public sector. We therefore clearly support the intentions of the proposal. And no, it is not too expensive to put an end to the pay hierarchy laid down in the official reform. For too long, equality has had to be put aside for the sake of labour market structures that we have laid down. And no, it is not a no-go for us, as politicians, to interfere in the debate on equality and to put an end to official reform. It is a political responsibility that we need to take on, and it can only go too slowly. Let me be quite clear: it does not seem that there is support for the citizens' proposal, unfortunately. So I would like to promise you, who have tabled this citizens' proposal, that the Free Greens will withdraw their own proposal, which we pulled from today's agenda, from the drawer again and again, until we get rid of this fundamental inequality. Thank you for the floor.
